# Verify proposed fixes to LR explanations

Another worker proposed edits to LSAT U's LR Question Explanations. You are the independent check. Nothing is applied unless you approve it. Be skeptical: your job is to catch edits that are wrong, not to admire them.

## Inputs
- The proposals: a JSON list at the path you are given. Each has qid, table, row_id, column, style, letter (for notes), old, new, kind, why, evidence.
- Read-only mirror of the database: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/` (`lr_questions.json` has stimulus, prompt = stem, correct_choice; `lr_answer_choices.json` has choice_text, tag per letter; `lr_explanations.json`, `lr_explanation_steps.json`, `lr_explanation_choice_notes.json`).
- The bank students read: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu/public/data/PT{n}-S{s}-LR.json`.
Load JSON with Python (`python -I`), never whole files into context. Write scripts only in your work directory.

## For every proposal
Read the question's stimulus, stem, all five choices, the key, and the old and new text in full. Then decide:
- **approve** — the new text is correct: every quote matches the choice or stimulus exactly, the logic is right for this question and key, the verdict for the letter agrees with the key (correct letter is argued as correct, wrong letters as wrong), nothing outside the defect was damaged, and it reads clearly.
- **fix** — the edit is the right idea but has a mistake you can correct. Give the full corrected `new`.
- **reject** — the edit is wrong, unnecessary, or makes things worse. Say why.

Hard rules for approve/fix: no added em dashes; no added gendered pronouns (he, she, his, her...) for people in the stimulus; no mention of Logic Games; never change which choice is credited; a wrong-answer note must say why that choice is wrong, a correct-answer note why it is right.

Also settle any open question the proposer raised in its `why` (for example a disputed verdict). If you cannot settle it with confidence, reject that edit and say what is needed.

## Output
Write JSON Lines to the output path you are given, one per proposal, in input order:
`{"row_id": "...", "qid": "...", "verdict": "approve|fix|reject", "new": "<only when fix: full corrected text>", "note": "one sentence"}`

Reply with counts of approve, fix, reject, and list every reject and fix with its one-sentence reason.

"""Mechanical checks on a proposals JSONL: old matches mirror, no new em dashes or gendered pronouns, sane edits."""
import json, sys, re, collections, difflib
DB = 'C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/'
steps = {r['id']: r for r in json.load(open(DB + 'lr_explanation_steps.json', encoding='utf-8'))}
notes = {r['id']: r for r in json.load(open(DB + 'lr_explanation_choice_notes.json', encoding='utf-8'))}
tables = {'lr_explanation_steps': steps, 'lr_explanation_choice_notes': notes}
GENDER = re.compile(r"\b(he|she|him|her|his|hers|himself|herself)\b", re.I)
c = collections.Counter(); problems = []; edits = []
seen = {}
for line in open(sys.argv[1], encoding='utf-8'):
    if not line.strip(): continue
    p = json.loads(line)
    if 'skip' in p: c['skip'] += 1; continue
    if 'flag' in p: c['flag'] += 1; continue
    c['edit'] += 1
    t = tables.get(p['table'])
    key = (p['table'], p['row_id'], p['column'])
    if t is None or p['column'] not in ('body', 'label'): problems.append(('bad table/column', p['qid'], key)); continue
    row = t.get(p['row_id'])
    if row is None: problems.append(('row missing', p['qid'], key)); continue
    if row[p['column']] != p['old']: problems.append(('old != mirror', p['qid'], key))
    if p['old'] == p['new']: problems.append(('no-op', p['qid'], key))
    if key in seen: problems.append(('duplicate row', p['qid'], key))
    seen[key] = p
    new_dash = p['new'].count('\u2014') - p['old'].count('\u2014')
    if new_dash > 0: problems.append(('adds em dash', p['qid'], key))
    og = len(GENDER.findall(p['old'])); ng = len(GENDER.findall(p['new']))
    if ng > og: problems.append(('adds gendered pronoun', p['qid'], key))
    if not p['new'].strip(): problems.append(('empty new', p['qid'], key))
    ratio = difflib.SequenceMatcher(None, p['old'], p['new']).ratio()
    edits.append((ratio, p))
print(dict(c), 'problems', len(problems))
for pr in problems: print('  ', pr)
if len(sys.argv) > 2:
    json.dump([p for _, p in edits], open(sys.argv[2], 'w', encoding='utf-8'), ensure_ascii=False)

import json,re,sys
DB='C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/'
L=lambda f: json.load(open(DB+f+'.json',encoding='utf-8'))
Q=L('lr_questions');A=L('lr_answer_choices');E=L('lr_explanations');S=L('lr_explanation_steps');N=L('lr_explanation_choice_notes')
P=json.load(open('../edits-C.json',encoding='utf-8'))
qids=[]
for p in P:
  if p['qid'] not in qids: qids.append(p['qid'])
qby={q['qid']:q for q in Q}
rows={}
for r in S: rows[r['id']]=('steps',r)
for r in N: rows[r['id']]=('notes',r)
out={}
for qid in qids:
  q=qby[qid]
  ch=sorted([a for a in A if a['question_id']==q['id']],key=lambda a:a['letter'])
  ex=[e for e in E if e['question_id']==q['id']]
  s=f"=== {qid} type={q['question_type']} key={q['correct_choice']}\nSTIM: {q['stimulus']}\nSTEM: {q['prompt']}\n"
  for a in ch: s+=f"  ({a['letter']}) [{a['tag']}] {a['choice_text']}\n"
  for e in ex:
    s+=f" -- explanation style={e['style']}\n"
    for st in sorted([x for x in S if x['explanation_id']==e['id']],key=lambda x:x['position']):
      s+=f"   step{st['position']} [{st['label']}] {st['body']}\n"
    for n in sorted([x for x in N if x['explanation_id']==e['id']],key=lambda x:x['letter']):
      s+=f"   note {n['letter']}: {n['body']}\n"
  s+="\n### PROPOSALS\n"
  for i,p in enumerate(P):
    if p['qid']!=qid: continue
    t,r=rows.get(p['row_id'],(None,None))
    cur=r[p['column']] if r else None
    s+=f"\n[#{i}] row={p['row_id']} {p['table']}.{p['column']} style={p['style']} letter={p['letter']} kind={p['kind']} old_matches_db={cur==p['old']}\n OLD: {p['old']}\n NEW: {p['new']}\n WHY: {p['why']}\n EVID: {p['evidence']}\n"
    new=p['new'] or ''; old=p['old'] or ''
    if new.count('\u2014')>old.count('\u2014'): s+=" !!EMDASH ADDED\n"
    for w in ['he','she','his','her','him','hers','himself','herself']:
      if len(re.findall(r'\b'+w+r'\b',new,re.I))>len(re.findall(r'\b'+w+r'\b',old,re.I)): s+=f" !!PRONOUN {w}\n"
    if re.search('logic game',new,re.I): s+=" !!LG\n"
    src=q['stimulus']+' '+' '.join(a['choice_text'] for a in ch)+' '+q['prompt']
    for m in re.findall(r'"([^"]+)"|\u201c([^\u201d]+)\u201d',new):
      m=m[0] or m[1]
      if m.strip(' .,') not in src and m.strip(' .,').lower() not in src.lower(): s+=f" ?quote not found: {m}\n"
  out[qid]=s
json.dump(out,open('dump.json','w',encoding='utf-8'),ensure_ascii=False)
for i,qid in enumerate(qids): print(i,qid)
import json,sys
d=json.load(open('dump.json',encoding='utf-8'));k=list(d)

STEM: The argument is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?
  (A) [Correct] It draws a conclusion about a population from observations of a subgroup that is quite likely to be unrepresentative of that population in certain relevant respects.
  (B) [Not Assumed] It takes for granted that most individuals do not vary significantly in the opinions they would express on a given issue if surveyed regarding that issue on different occasions.
  (C) [Wrong Sampling Flaw] It relies on the accuracy of a survey made under conditions in which it is probable that most of the responses to that survey did not correctly reflect the opinions of the respondents.
  (D) [Reversed Conclusion / Evidence Match] It uses evidence about an opinion held by the majority of a population in an attempt to justify a conclusion regarding the opinion of a small part of that population.
  (E) [Out of Scope: influenced opinions] It takes for granted that the fund-raising letter had some influence on the opinions of most of the people who received it.

=== PT111-S1-Q17 type=Flaw key=E
STIM: On the basis of the available evidence, Antarctica has generally been thought to have been covered by ice for at least the past 14 million years. Recently, however, three-million-year-old fossils of a kind previously found only in ocean-floor sediments were discovered under the ice sheet covering central Antarctica. About three million years ago, therefore, the Antarctic ice sheet must temporarily have melted. After all, either severe climatic warming or volcanic activity in Antarctica's mountains could have melted the ice sheet, thus raising sea levels and submerging the continent.
STEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to which one of the following criticisms?
  (A) [Bad Premise Match] That a given position is widely believed to be true is taken to show that the position in question must, in fact, be true.
  (B) [Bad Premise/Conclusion Match] That either of two things could independently have produced a given effect is taken to show that those two things could not have operated in conjunction to produce that effect.
  (C) [Bad Description] Establishing that a certain event occurred is confused with having established the cause of that event.
  (D) [Wrong Flaw] A claim that has a very general application is based entirely on evidence from a narrowly restricted range of cases.
  (E) [Correct] An inconsistency that, as presented, has more than one possible resolution is treated as though only one resolution is possible.

### PROPOSALS

 OLD: *Given that ..* there's only evidence that Z was sane *after* the shooting, not during the shooting.
  *How can we argue that ...* Z was **not** insane at the time he fired the shot (i.e. he was sane)?
  Well, what is the evidence he was that we was insane? Z's attorney hasn't presented any evidence to support the contention that Z was insane at the time of the shooting.
  The fact that we don't have evidence Z was sane at the time of the shooting is not therefore evidence that Z was insane. After all, that would work in reverse too. The fact that Z's attorney has provided no evidence that Z was insane at the time of the shooting would, by the same logic, be evidence that Z was sane.
  A lack of evidence for X does not prove or even necessarily suggest that X is false.
  To think this way is to commit one of the 10 famous flaws, *Unproven vs. Proven False.*

=== PT122-S2-Q23 type=Flaw key=B
STIM: Commentator: Human behavior cannot be fully understood without inquiring into nonphysical aspects of persons. As evidence of this, I submit the following: suppose that we had a complete scientific account of the physical aspects of some particular human action—every neurological, physiological, and environmental event involved. Even with all that we would obviously still not truly comprehend the action or know why it occurred.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument's reasoning?
  (A) [Too Strong: only superficially / Out of Scope: analogy] No support is offered for its conclusion other than an analogy that relates only superficially to the issue at hand.
  (B) [Correct] The purported evidence that it cites in support of its conclusion presumes that the conclusion is true.
  (C) [Wrong Flaw: Unproven vs. Proven False] It concludes that a proposition must be true merely on the grounds that it has not been proven false.
  (D) [Not a Flaw] It fails to indicate whether the speaker is aware of any evidence that could undermine the conclusion.
  (E) [Too Strong: any] It presumes, without providing justification, that science can provide a complete account of any physical phenomenon.

 OLD: This argument is about whether or not we can fully understand human behavior using only physical descriptions, or whether to fully understand human behavior you would need some nonphysical information too (like their thoughts).
This is essentially the Mind / Body problem from philosophy. Are humans reducible to our constituent physical processes, or is there something like a Mind / Soul that is not captured by our physical properties? (I even wrote )
This author is arguing that we aren't reducible to our physical parts. There's more to us than just the physical explanation of what our bodies are doing. He says that if we had a complete physical account, we still wouldn't truly comprehend the action or know why it occurred.
If you were a materialist or determinist who thought that humans are reducible to physical terms what would you say in response?
We would probably just say, "I disagree. I think we would truly comprehend the action. After all, it is a **complete** explanation of every neurological, physiological, and environmental event involved."

[#8] row=66e5fe43-9e0e-48c3-9cf2-074e132a1b96 lr_explanation_steps.body style=single letter=None kind=transcription old_matches_db=True
 OLD: Whenever we're doing Flaw and we see any conditional logic in the premises, we immediately start assuming we'll going to probably see a Necessary vs. Sufficient flaw (where the author supplies a conditional relationship or two in the evidence, but then applies that rule in some backwards or opposite manner to reach the conclusion).
The first sentence has "in order to X, you must Y". The second has "doing A will lead to B".
The conclusion is also a pseudo-conditional, because it's saying "something that is P can be assumed to be Q".
So we might need to diagram these if we're trying to figure out exactly where the author might be making an illegal reversal or negation.
Prem 1: maintain high S.O.L → maintain functioning Inf
Prem 2: major invest in improving Inf → rise in S.O.L
Do those two premises chain together? No, not really. To chain, you need an overlapping term. "Standard of living" is an overlapping term, but we're talking about it in two different ways -- whether or not you're maintaining a **high** standard of living / whether or not your standard of living has risen **higher** than it previously was.
If those were the same we could make this chain: major invest → high S.O.L → maintain in impr Inf. funct. Inf
But they're not. You could raise your standard of living from 150th in the world to 140th. It would be **higher** but it wouldn't be a **high** standard of living that you're maintaining.
Okay, so the conditional premises don't chain together. So is there a conditional logic problem with the conclusion? Yes. The conclusion looks like this: S.O.L is rising → Invested heavily in imp Inf
That's an illegal reversal of the 2nd sentence: invest heavily in imp Inf → rise in S.O.L.
We were told that major investments would lead to a rise in S.O.L.
But other things might also lead to a rise in S.O.L (better daycare and family leave options / cleaner environments / industrial ingenuity creating new jobs and wealth like in Silicon Valley).
If a country's standard of living is on the rise, we can't assume it's because of major infrastructure investment. It might be because of one of these other things that cause standard of living to rise.

[#11] row=31d897ff-4a76-4e0e-ad38-7d95749cd77a lr_explanation_steps.body style=single letter=None kind=slip old_matches_db=True
 OLD: We're doing a Flaw question, and we see conditional logic indicators (any, only). What should we be thinking? We should deeply suspect that this is going to commit the #1 Famous Flaw, Necessary vs. Sufficient, which just means that the author presents a conditional logic premise and then botches using it by making an illegal reversal or negation.
The author draws two conclusions, so we should look at each one.
The first (intermediate) conclusion is that there must not be abundant gnats. Why? Because there are no geckos, and geckos are found in every environment with abundant gnats.
```
~Geckos → ~Lots of Gnats
Here = ~Geckos
Thus, Here = ~Lots of Gnats
```
This mini-argument seems airtight. It just applies a fact (no geckos) that triggers the contrapositive of the conditional in the first sentence (lots of gnats → geckos).
Okay the main conclusion was that the climate is not wet.
We were told that gnats survive only in a wet climate. Only / only if are necessary indicators (the only is sufficient), so the idea of "wet climate" goes on the right of the arrow.
```
Gnats → Wet climate
~Wet climate → ~Gnats
```
The author's move from the 2nd to last claim to the final claim is, "If there is not an abundant population of gnats, then it must not be a wet climate." But that move looks like this:
```
~Gnats → ~Wet climate
```
That's not what the rule said. It said "if yes gnats, then yes wet climate", but the author is trying to argue "if no gnats, then no wet climate". That's an illegal negation.

We were told that gnats survive only in a wet climate. Only / only if are necessary indicators (the idea that follows "only" is necessary, and the other idea is sufficient), so the idea of "wet climate" goes on the right of the arrow.

That's not what the rule said. It said "if yes gnats, then yes wet climate", but the author is trying to argue "if no gnats, then no wet climate". That's an illegal negation.
 WHY: The parenthetical seemed to call "only" a sufficient indicator; it now says the idea after "only" is necessary, which matches the diagram Gnats → Wet climate.
 EVID: Stimulus: "Gnats can survive only in wet climates."; diagram: "Gnats → Wet climate"

=== PT138-S2-Q24 type=Flaw key=E
STIM: Studies have found that human tears contain many of the same hormones that the human body produces in times of emotional stress. Hence, shedding tears removes significant quantities of these hormones from the body. Therefore, crying must have the effect of reducing emotional stress.
STEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument
  (A) [Not an Objection] overlooks the possibility that if crying has a tendency to reduce emotional stress, this tendency might arise because of something other than the shedding of tears
  (B) [Wrong Flaw] confuses a condition that is required for the production of a given phenomenon with a condition that in itself would be sufficient to cause the production of that phenomenon
  (C) [Bad Conclusion Match] fails to adequately address the possibility that, even if one phenomenon causally contributes to a second phenomenon, the second phenomenon may causally influence the first as well
  (D) [Out of Scope: joint causes] fails to adequately distinguish between two distinct factors that are jointly responsible for causing a given phenomenon
  (E) [Correct] takes for granted that because certain substances are present whenever a condition occurs, those substances are a cause of that condition

### PROPOSALS

  (A) [Out of Scope: consequences] misrepresents a criticism about the consequences of a practice as a criticism about the intrinsic value of the practice
  (B) [Bad Premise Match] takes for granted that a certain authority should be respected merely because it is an authority
  (C) [Bad Premise Match] takes for granted that a particular practice is good simply because it is the way things have traditionally been done

 OLD: This describes one response to the famous Causal flaw. This argument had no causal interpretations happening. The author wasn't trying to explain what was causing overzealous ticketing. She was just defending its existence.

Note: this formulation of the Causal flaw answer is always wrong, because the answer is sure of itself. We can never definitively say that the author confused cause for effect. We can only point out the possibility that she failed to consider that possibility.

281	=== PT108-S3-Q23 type=Most Strongly Supported key=B
282	STIM: Commentator: A political constitution that provides the framework for the laws of a nation must be interpreted to apply to new situations not envisioned by its authors. Although these interpretations express the moral and political beliefs of the interpreters, they are usually portrayed as embodying the intentions of the authors. This fiction is vital because without it the illusion, so necessary for political stability, that laws are the bequest of a long tradition rather than the preferences of contemporary politicians would vanish.
283	STEM: Which one of the following is most strongly supported by the commentator's statements, if they are true?
284	  (A) [Authors vs. Leaders] If the people of a nation do not believe that the laws under which they live express the intentions of their political leaders, that nation will become more politically unstable.
285	  (B) [Correct] Political instability will increase if the people of a nation cease to believe that their constitution is being interpreted consistently with the intentions of its authors.
286	  (C) [Too Strong: divergence of beliefs] Political instability will ensue if people come to believe there is a divergence between the beliefs of the authors of their constitution and those of their present political leaders.
287	  (D) [Out of Scope: written constitution Wrong Causal Factor] A written constitution preserves the illusion that laws are the bequest of a long tradition rather than the creations of modern politicians.
288	  (E) [Unsupported Causal Connection] The perceived lack of a long legal tradition in a nation makes the political stability of that nation dependent upon the fiction that its present political leaders share the intentions of the authors of the constitution of that nation.
289	
290	### PROPOSALS
291

399	STIM: Science teacher: In any nation, a flourishing national scientific community is essential to a successful economy. For such a community to flourish requires that many young people become excited enough about science that they resolve to become professional scientists. Good communication between scientists and the public is necessary to spark that excitement.
400	STEM: The science teacher's statements provide the most support for which one of the following?
401	  (A) [Reversed Logic] If scientists communicate with the public, many young people will become excited enough about science to resolve to become professional scientists.
402	  (B) [Relative vs. Absolute One-Claim Support] The extent to which a national scientific community flourishes depends principally on the number of young people who become excited enough about science to resolve to become professional scientists.
403	  (C) [Correct] No nation can have a successful economy unless at some point scientists have communicated well with the public.
404	  (D) [Too Strong: Most] It is essential to any nation's economy that most of the young people in that nation who are excited about science become professional scientists.
405	  (E) [Too Strong: any scientific endeavor] An essential component of success in any scientific endeavor is good communication between the scientists involved in that endeavor and the public.

2	STIM: In an experiment, subjects were shown a series of images on a computer screen, appearing usually at the top but occasionally at the bottom. Subjects were asked to guess each time where the next image would appear on the screen. They guessed correctly less than half of the time. The subjects all reported that they based their guesses on patterns they believed they saw in the sequence. Instead, if they had simply guessed that the next image would always appear at the top, they would have been correct most of the time.
3	STEM: If all of the statements above are true, which one of the following must also be true?
4	  (A) [Unsupported Relationship] If the subjects had always guessed that the next image would appear at the top, they would not have been basing their guesses on any pattern they believed they saw in the sequence.
5	  (B) [Too Broad / Strong] Basing one's guesses about what will happen next on the basis of patterns one believes one sees is less likely to lead to correct guesses than always guessing that what has happened before will happen next.
6	  (C) [Too Strong: no pattern] There was no predictable pattern that one could reasonably believe occurred in the series of images on the computer screen.
7	  (D) [Correct] Some of the subjects sometimes guessed that the next image would appear at the bottom of the computer screen, but were incorrect.
8	  (E) [Out of Scope: rational / Too Strong: most rational] The most rational strategy for guessing correctly where the next image would appear would have been simply to always guess that the image would appear at the top.

This suggests that life may be able to arise under many difficult conditions throughout the universe.
3	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes the role played in the biologist's argument by the claim that the fossilized bacteria discovered in rocks 3.5 billion years old must have had a long evolutionary history?
4	  (A) [Contradicted: no support provided] It is a claim for which no support is provided in the argument, and that is used to illustrate the conclusion of the argument as a whole.
5	  (B) [Contradicted: no support provided] It is a claim for which no support is provided in the argument, and that is used to support a claim that in turn lends support to the conclusion of the argument as a whole.

7	  (D) [Bad 2nd Half] It is a claim for which some support is provided in the argument, and that itself is not used to support any other claim in the argument.
8	  (E) [Bad 2nd Half] It is a claim for which some support is provided in the argument, and that itself is used to support two distinct conclusions, neither of which is intended to provide support for the other.

166	  (D) [Correct] It is an allegedly untenable consequence of a view rejected in the argument's overall conclusion.
167	  (E) [No Intermediate Conclusions] It is a premise offered in support of an intermediate conclusion of the argument.

158	 OLD: Let's look for something like, "they're arguing about whether Stalin's vindictiveness in his later years were something new and noteworthy vs. something that had been going on for a while" or "whether his vindictiveness in his later years is compelling vs. weak evidence for a loss in sanity".
159	p.s. glasnost is a Russian word meaning "openness", that also was a shorthand for describing the end of the Soviet Union / Cold War in the late 1980s, under Mikhail Gorbachev. The other word from that time, perestroika, meant "change". It was sort of a transition period where Gorbachev tried to move Russia out of its Soviet Union, imperial, communist empire into a slightly more democratic, more Western, more worldly entity. So "writers under glasnost" would have had the motivation of being apologists for how scandalously cruel the Stalin regime was. They would be trying to make Russia not seem as crazy as Stalin would make it look on the world stage, and thus "explain away" his atrocities by saying he was just a confused old man losing his sanity, not the logical conclusion of an authoritarian communist system.

81	STIM: In order to pressure the government of Country S to become less repressive, some legislators in Country R want to ban all exports from R to S. Companies in R that manufacture telecommunication equipment such as telephones and fax machines have argued that exports of their products should be exempted from the ban, on the grounds that it is impossible for a country to remain repressive when telecommunication equipment is widely available to the population of that country.
82	STEM: Which one of the following is an assumption on which the argument given by the manufacturers depends?
83	  (A) [Out of Scope: recently increased] The government of S has recently increased the amount of telecommunication equipment it allows to be imported into the country.
84	  (B) [Correct] The telecommunication equipment that would be imported into S if the exemption were to be granted would not be available solely to top government officials in S.
85	  (C) [Weakens, if anything Too Strong: a majority] A majority of the members of R’s legislature do not favor exempting telecommunication equipment from the ban on exports to Country S.
86	  (D) [Too Strong: of all / the most] Of all exports that could be sent to Country S, telecommunication equipment would be the most effective in helping citizens of S oppose that country’s repressive government.
87	  (E) [Too Strong: indefinitely] Without pressure from Country R, the government of S would be able to continue repressing its citizens indefinitely.

68	STIM: We are in a new industrial revolution that requires management trainees to develop “action learning” from real experience within business and industry, rather than getting tied up with theory and academia. Business schools seem unable, on their own, to tear themselves away from their largely academic roots and move closer to the realities of today’s business and industry; too often, trainees in business schools find themselves studying hypothetical cases instead of real ones. Furthermore, business schools have been slow to respond to the needs of business. Therefore, business schools should allow business executives to set curricula for management trainees that could then be taught by academics.
69	STEM: The argument relies on which one of the following assumptions?
70	  (A) [Too Strong: no] Academics in business schools have no practical business experience that is valuable.

72	  (C) [Too Strong: incapable Weakens] Academics are not capable of teaching curricula suitable for relevant management training.
73	  (D) [Flip a Fact Unknown Comparison] Academic training outside of business schools is more responsive to the needs of business than is training within business schools.
74	  (E) [Correct] Today’s business executives have valuable insight into business that academics in business schools do not have.
